    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. 1. **PID 587160 (GPU 1)**: Process doesn't exist anymore (ps returned exit code 1). But it's still holding 80GB on GPU 1. This is a zombie GPU allocation - the process died but GPU memory wasn't released.

2. **PID 564905 (GPU 0)**: Running `plan018-additive-random-b01-s44` (random β=0.1 seed=44), running for ~1h 3m. This looks legitimate.

3. **SFT checkpoint**: Exists at `/root/autodl-tmp/outputs/plan009_sft_baseline_s42/checkpoint-500/` with model files + 16GB optimizer.pt.
   
The core issue is that GPU 1 is completely blocked by a zombie process holding 80GB of memory, which is why the eval worker and plan006 both failed trying to allocate GPU resources. I need to escalate this resource leak to the Director and also check which GPUs the project is configured to use. Let me also check if there are any tmux sessions or parent processes still holding onto that GPU allocation, since the zombie process itself is already gone. I should verify the constraint configuration to make sure GPU assignments are actually correct before recommending a reset.

_source line 49 · 2026-05-14T12:38:19.429Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full picture:

1. **PID 587155** (plan006-prolog-exec-s43, killed) - process dead
2. **PID 587160** (on GPU 1, 80GB) - process dead but GPU memory not released  
3. **PID 572604** (plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4) - process dead (training completed)

But GPU 1 still shows 79971 MiB used by PID 587160. This PID doesn't exist anymore as a Linux process. This is a classic NVIDIA driver zombie memory leak.
 
PID 587160 was likely spawned as a child of 587155, and when the parent got killed, the child failed to properly release its GPU memory, leaving it orphaned. This orphaned GPU memory is now blocking everything downstream—the eval for plan009-sft-r4 that just failed, plan006-prolog-exec-s43-r2 which hit OOM at step 0, and all the queued experiments waiting to run.

GPU 0 is legitimately occupied by plan018-additive-random-b01-s44, which has been running for about an hour at 46% utilization. I'm checking whether the GPU allocation constraints are properly configured, but since there are only two GPUs and both are in use for this project, the setup appears reasonable. The critical issue here is the zombie process holding 80GB on GPU 1—it's no longer running but still blocking all downstream work.

The zombie allocation on GPU 1 is preventing plan009-sft-r4's eval from running and caused plan006-prolog-exec-s43-r2 to OOM. I need to kill this orphaned process, likely through nvidia-smi or by hunting down any child processes still attached to PID 587160. The OOM failure isn't a real resource problem—it's just a symptom of the blocked GPU. I should also note that plan009-sft-r4's training completed successfully with the checkpoint saved, so the eval worker failure is purely due to GPU contention, not a training issue.
```

plan016 (1000步) | Change |\\\\n|--------|-------------------|-----------------|--------|\\\\n| eval PR | 0.93 | 0.96 | +3.2% |\\\\n| eval ISR | 0.123 | 0.0027 | -97.8% |\\\\n| proved_only ISR | 0.132 | 0.0028 | -97.9% |\\\\n| components | 6.58 | 18.55 | +182% |\\\\n| ISR=0 ratio | - | 94.8% | - |\\\\n| n_necessary=0 ratio | - | 94.8% | - |\\\\n\\\\n## Key findings:\\\\n1. PR improves (+3%) but ISR collapses near-zero (0.0027). The model gets BETTER at proving while becoming WORSE at faithful reasoning.\\\\n2. Component count nearly tripled (6.58→18.55). Model generates longer proofs with more components, but 94.8% have ZERO necessary components.\\\\n3. NOT tautological collapse (comp=2 ratio=0). Instead: contradiction-based collapse — model embeds P(a) and ¬P(a) among many irrelevant premises, making anything provable via explosion.\\\\n4. Windowed ISR trend is monotonically decreasing: 0.1115 (101-200) → 0.0433 (401-500) → 0.0200 (601-700) → 0.0144 (901-1000). No recovery signal.\\\\n5. The exec-only reward creates a pure reward hacking incentive: the model discovers that contradictions + trivially true conclusions always satisfy the prover.\\\\n\\\\n## Implication for paper:\\\\nExtended training is a clean ablation showing exec-only reward's failure mode worsens over time. - Model: Qwen3-4B, 500 steps, seed=44, data/train_500_seed42.jsonl


- Ran on westd-39279 with cuda:0 for 2.06 hours

The training completed all 500 steps with parse_rate at 1.0 and prove_rate at 0.647, though the entropy metrics are concerning—dropping to 0.02-0.04 indicates the policy has collapsed significantly. The final ISR sits at 0.081, and in the later steps the prove_rate bounced around between 0.625-1.0 while ISR ranged from 0.028-0.2. Evaluation hasn't started yet.

Looking at the comparison runs with the same seed, I'm seeing a pattern: the exec-only variant hit 98% prove rate but fell into tautological collapse with 94% of solutions being 2-component, while the additive variant with ISR β=0.1 achieved 89% prove rate but shows conclusion duplication hacking. Across the random reward variants with β=0.1, seed 42 got 79% prove rate with 0.104 ISR and 8.46 components, seed 43 failed due to disk space at step 200, and seed 44 just finished training and is waiting for evaluation.

Now I'm verifying the full spec for this run—data path, count, model, seed, reward formula, and step count all check out, though the checkpoint path hasn't been recorded yet since we're still in the analysis phase. GPU allocation looks clean with cuda:0 available and properly assigned, and the training curve is starting to show the prove rate climbing from its initial low point.

The metrics tell an interesting story: prove rate reaches about 0.65 by the end, which is lower than the seed 42 run but not drastically so, while ISR stays quite low at 0.08. The entropy does collapse down to that 0.02-0.04 range, which is typical for GRPO training. The run completed without crashes or memory issues, so the spec is solid. The real question is whether this seed sensitivity in the training metrics will persist through evaluation—I'll need to see how it compares against the planned baseline once eval finishes.
